=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given integer sequences of length N: A=(A_1,A_2,\cdots,A_N) and B=(B_1,B_2,\cdots,B_N), and an integer K.
You can perform the following operation zero or more times.

- Choose integers i and j (1 \leq i,j \leq N).
Here, |i-j| \leq K must hold.
Then, change the value of A_i to A_j.

Determine whether it is possible to make A identical to B.
There are T test cases for each input.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
case_1
case_2
\vdots
case_T

Each test case is given in the following format:
N K
A_1 A_2 \cdots A_N
B_1 B_2 \cdots B_N

Output

For each test case, print Yes if it is possible to make A identical to B, and No otherwise.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 125000
- 1 \leq K < N \leq 250000
- 1 \leq A_i,B_i \leq N
- The sum of N across all test cases in each input is at most 250000.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4
3 1
1 1 2
1 2 2
5 4
2 4 5 1 3
2 1 3 2 2
13 1
3 1 3 3 5 3 3 4 2 2 2 5 1
5 3 3 3 4 2 2 2 2 5 5 1 3
20 14
10 6 6 19 13 16 15 15 2 10 2 16 9 12 2 6 13 5 5 9
5 9 6 2 10 19 16 15 13 12 10 2 9 6 5 16 19 12 15 13

Sample Output 1

Yes
Yes
No
Yes

Consider the first test case.
If we operate with i=2 and j=3, the value of A_2 will be changed to A_3=2, resulting in A=(1,2,2).

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Voter model on a graph: There is a dual process of coalescing random walks. Given a set of final vertices, their ancestral paths are random walks that move along edges backward in time. The final ancestry map f is such that for any two final vertices u,v, if their paths coalesce then f(u)=f(v). If not, they remain distinct. In 1D with long-range jumps, paths can cross without meeting if jumps over each other. Thus order can change. The only constraint is that paths are walks on G; any assignment f can be realized by choosing paths from each final vertex to f(v) that do not intersect except maybe if same f? But paths can cross. Since G is connected, we can choose paths independently; but they share time and can interact? In dual, if two lineages are at same vertex at same time, they coalesce and thereafter same. If we design paths, we must avoid unwanted coalescence. Is it always possible to connect each final vertex to an arbitrary initial vertex of correct color by vertex-disjoint (in time) paths? Not always; sample3 maybe due to path packing.

Sample3 seems designed to violate a "cut" condition. Let's compute for each prefix/suffix maybe.

For K=1, operation on adjacent cells. There is a known invariant involving "for every cut between i and i+1, the multiset of colors on one side can change by moving a color across the cut only if that color exists on both sides?" Actually copying across an edge can transfer a color across the cut without moving a token? Let's examine cut between positions i and i+1. Operation across this edge can copy value from right to left or left to right. If we copy right value v to left position i, then v appears on left side; count of v on left increases by1, count of old u on left decreases by1. The right side count of v unchanged. Thus a color can cross the cut from right to left by duplication, without decreasing its count on right. Similarly left to right. So colors can cross cuts freely if they exist on one side. But to cross, the target side must have some color to overwrite; that color may disappear from that side. This is like multiset exchange across cut: one item from left side changes to a color present on right side. Thus across a cut, the multiset of colors on the left can be transformed by replacing one left color with any color present on the right. This is exactly the complete graph multiset reachability between the two sides, with the additional constraint that the source color must be present on the other side. Since operations across the cut can be repeated. This might yield a local condition for each cut!

For K=1, only adjacent edges. Consider any cut between i and i+1. Operations not crossing this cut cannot change the multiset of colors on the left side? Wait operations entirely within left side can change colors using sources within left side; they can change multiset on left but not exchange with right. Operations crossing the cut are the only way to introduce colors from right into left or vice versa. But operations within left side can also eliminate colors, etc. However if we look at the set of colors present on the left side, can it shrink/grow? A color can appear on left only if it is already on left or copied from right across cut. Thus for any prefix [1..i], the set of colors present in the prefix can only gain colors from the suffix (via cut) and lose colors by overwriting within prefix. It cannot gain a color not present in the whole array. So support(prefix) can expand to include suffix colors. But maybe there is a condition on the first time a color appears in prefix.

Similarly, consider the multiset of colors in a prefix. Operations within prefix can transform it according to complete graph rules with source set = colors present in prefix. Operations crossing cut can replace a prefix color by a suffix color, effectively adding that suffix color to prefix multiset and removing a prefix color. This is like we can move colors across cut from suffix to prefix, but not from prefix to suffix? Wait operation can also copy left value to right, replacing a suffix color by prefix color, so colors can move from prefix to suffix too. Thus across cut, both directions possible: replace a left color by a right color (right color crosses left) or replace a right color by a left color (left color crosses right). So multiset on each side can exchange elements: one item from left changes to a color from right, or vice versa. This is exactly we can swap a "token" of color? It changes counts: left loses u gains v; right unchanged. So total count of v increases, u decreases. This is not a swap of tokens; it's copying.

Let's characterize reachability via "last update times" and "source graph".

Let t_i be the last time position i is updated (or 0 if never). For each i with A_i != B_i, t_i >0. At time t_i, position i is set to value of some neighbor s_i. Since this is the last update of i, after t_i, i remains B_i. The source s_i at time t_i has value B_i. If s_i is updated after t_i, its value may change; but at t_i it must have B_i. Thus s_i must have acquired B_i at some time <= t_i, and not changed between that time and t_i. We can define a directed edge i -> s_i (target i depends on source s_i). This forms a directed graph on positions with outdegree 1 for changed positions, 0 for never changed? Actually if A_i = B_i, t_i=0, no dependency; it already has final value from initial. It can still be used as source. For changed positions, edge to source. Since time decreases along edges? Source s_i's acquisition time of B_i is <= t_i. If s_i is also changed to B_i later? Wait s_i has value B_i at time t_i. If s_i's final value is not B_i, then s_i must be updated after t_i to change away. Thus s_i's last update time t_{s_i} > t_i. So edge i -> s_i goes from lower last-update time to higher last-update time. If s_i's final value is B_i, then s_i may have t_{s_i}=0 or <t_i? Actually if final B_{s_i}=B_i and s_i initially not B_i, it must be updated at t_{s_i} to B_i; to be source at time t_i, need t_{s_i} <= t_i. If t_{s_i}=t_i? Can't update both same time. If t_{s_i} < t_i, then edge i->s_i has t_i > t_{s_i}, so time decreases along edge. If s_i initially B_i, t_{s_i}=0 < t_i. Thus for sources whose final value is B_i, time decreases. For sources that later change away, time increases. So dependency graph not simply acyclic by time.

Maybe use "pebble game" on intervals. Operation with distance K means a value can be copied from p to q if |p-q|<=K. Maybe we can think of the process in terms of "we can choose a sequence of colors to delete". Operation deletes one occurrence of color u (at target) and creates one occurrence of color v (source). Thus total number of occurrences of each color changes by +1 for v, -1 for u. This is a multiset reaction. The spatial constraint: to create v at position i, there must be an occurrence of v within distance K at that time. But if we ignore space, multiset reachability is as complete graph. Spatial constraint means a color can only be created at positions within K of some current occurrence of that color. Thus the set of positions occupied by color v can expand by at most K per creation. Over time, v can occupy any connected component of the graph from initial positions. Since graph connected, if v exists, it can eventually occupy any position, provided we don't delete all v. Let's model dependencies between colors. Suppose final position i has color c. To set i to c, we need a source position p with color c at that time. Ultimately, the color c at p comes from an initial occurrence of c. If p is not an initial occurrence of c, it must have been set to c earlier using another source. Thus to create final c at i, we need a chain of positions from some initial c to i. The chain can use intermediate positions whose final colors are not c; those positions must later be set to their final colors, requiring their own chains. This creates a dependency graph between final positions: i (final c) depends on p (source). If p's final color is not c, then p depends on some source for its final color, and that source must be available after p is used for c. So p's finalization must occur after i's finalization? Actually if p is used to set i to c, and p later changes to d, then p's finalization (to d) occurs after i's finalization. Thus i depends on p being c at time t_i; p depends on its final source q at later time t_p. So in dependency graph, edge i -> p for color c, and p -> q for color d. This is a directed graph where each position (except maybe initially final) has outdegree 1 to its source for final color. There is no acyclicity requirement; cycles in time would be impossible because t_i < t_p < ... < t_i. But if p's final color is c, then p could be finalized before i, so edge i -> p has t_i > t_p, no cycle? Let's define directed edge from target i to source p (i depends on p having color B_i at time t_i). If B_p = B_i, then p's final color same; p can be finalized before i, so t_p < t_i. If B_p != B_i, p must be finalized after i, so t_p > t_i. Thus along edge i->p, if colors differ, time increases; if same, time decreases. A cycle would imply time both increase and decrease impossible if all edges in cycle have colors? Actually if cycle contains at least one edge with different colors, time increases along that edge and decreases along same-color edges? Let's compute: For edge i->p, if B_i != B_p then t_p > t_i (increase). If B_i = B_p then t_p < t_i (decrease). A directed cycle in this dependency graph would require sum of increases/decreases around cycle =0, but each edge imposes strict inequality; impossible because t values would have t_a > t_b > ... > t_a. Wait if same-color edge decreases (t_p < t_i), different-color edge increases (t_p > t_i). Both are inequalities; around a cycle, you get t_{v1} < t_{v2} < ... < t_{v1} if all edges? Let's orient edge from target to source. For edge u->v:
- if B_u != B_v: t_v > t_u (source later)
- if B_u = B_v: t_v < t_u (source earlier)
So along edge, t_v compared to t_u depends on color equality. A cycle can be consistent if it contains both types? Example u->v (diff, t_v>t_u), v->w (same, t_w<t_v), w->u (diff? t_u>t_w). Could have t_u=1,t_v=2,t_w=1.5? Not integer but possible. So cycles possible. Need not be acyclic.

Maybe choose t_i as last update; if B_u != B_v, source v must be updated after u (to change away from B_u). If B_u = B_v, source v can be updated before u (or never). So dependency graph is a "mixed" graph.

There is a dual process: The type at each final vertex is the type of an initial vertex reached by a coalescing random walk. The joint distribution of ancestral paths. For arbitrary sequence (not random), we can choose any system of coalescing paths. The reachability condition is: There exists a collection of paths in G from each final vertex i to some initial vertex a(i) with A_{a(i)}=B_i, such that if two paths intersect at the same time, they coalesce and thereafter same. Since we can schedule updates to realize any such path system? I think yes: Given a set of ancestral paths (coalescing), we can run updates backwards to create them. This is the standard duality. So the problem reduces to: Is there a set of coalescing paths on the time-expanded graph connecting final positions to initial positions of matching colors? Time is unbounded, so paths can be arbitrarily long and can wait. In continuous time, paths are random walks that can move along edges; they can cross without coalescing if they are at different times? Actually in the graphical representation, each update event is an oriented edge from source to target at a specific time. A lineage follows the arrow if at the target at that time. Two lineages coalesce if they are at the same vertex at the same time and then follow same arrows. We can design the graphical representation (schedule of updates) to realize any set of paths that are vertex-disjoint in time except coalescences. Since time unbounded, we can separate paths in time to avoid unwanted meetings. For example, to realize arbitrary ancestry map f, we can process each final vertex one by one at different times, moving its lineage from i to f(i) along a path, while keeping other lineages at their final vertices? But if we move one lineage, it may pass through vertices occupied by other lineages at that time, causing coalescence. We can schedule times so that only one lineage moves at a time, and other lineages are "parked" at their final vertices? But if a lineage passes through a vertex occupied by another lineage, they coalesce. To avoid, we need vertex-disjoint paths at each time. In 1D, paths can cross only by jumping over? If both lineages move, crossing possible. But with one lineage moving at a time, it cannot pass through a stationary lineage without coalescing. However it can jump over if edge length >1? For K=1, cannot jump over; so monotonicity. For K>1, can jump over stationary lineages if distance between positions <=K and no intermediate? Actually if stationary at pos2, lineage at pos1 wants to go to pos3 with K=2; edge 1-3 directly, jumps over pos2 without meeting. So crossing possible. Thus the ability to avoid coalescence depends on K and path packing.

This suggests a topological condition: We need to route paths from final positions to initial positions of matching colors in the graph G, with the constraint that paths cannot intersect at the same time, but can cross via long edges. Since time unbounded, we can use "elevated" paths; in 2D spacetime, paths are curves that move vertically (time) and horizontally along edges. They cannot intersect except coalesce. We need a collection of nonintersecting curves in the spacetime cylinder connecting bottom (initial) to top (final), with colors matching. This is like disjoint paths in a grid with horizontal jumps up to K. Since time dimension is continuous and we can add as many time layers as needed, we can always separate paths by assigning different time intervals, unless they need to cross in space? In 1D, two curves can cross in spacetime without intersecting if one goes over the other in time? Wait spacetime is 2D (position, time). Two paths from (i,0) to (a, T) and (j,0) to (b,T) with i<j but a>b (crossing in space) must cross in spacetime if they are continuous and cannot jump over each other. For K=1, paths move along adjacent edges; to exchange order, they must intersect, causing coalescence. Thus impossible unless same color. For K>1, paths can jump over each other via long edges, so crossing without intersection possible. But if K is limited, maybe paths can cross only if there is enough space? Actually a path from position i to a can jump over another path at position p if |i-a|<=K and p between; no interaction. So order can change arbitrarily if K>=2? Let's try to design an algorithm based on "we can always perform operations in a canonical order: for i from 1 to N, make A_i = B_i by copying from some position j with A_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K, then never touch i again." If we process left to right and never touch finalized positions, can we always reach B if possible? This is like "online" construction. At step i, positions <i are fixed to B and won't change. We need to set A_i to B_i using a source j with |i-j|<=K that currently has B_i. Since positions <i are fixed, source j could be <i (already final) or >=i. If source j < i, it has B_i and is within K; then copying from it to i sets A_i=B_i without changing source. If source j >= i, it currently has B_i; copying to i sets i, source unchanged. So as long as there is an occurrence of B_i within distance K of i at that time, we can fix i. But after fixing i, we may have changed A_i from old value, potentially destroying the only occurrence of some other value needed later. However if that old value is needed later, it might still exist elsewhere. This greedy may fail if we destroy a unique source. But maybe we can choose order to avoid.

This is analogous to "topological sorting" of dependencies: To set i to B_i, we need a source of B_i within K. If the source is i itself (A_i=B_i), no operation. If source is some j that currently has B_i, and j is not finalized yet, we can use it. We should finalize positions in an order such that when finalizing i, the source j either is already finalized with B_i (so safe) or will be finalized later and currently has B_i. If j is not finalized and its final value is not B_i, then using it now is okay; it will be changed later. But if j's current value B_i is the only occurrence and j is needed as source for its own final value later, using it now doesn't destroy it (copying doesn't change source), so okay. The danger is destroying the old value at i, not source. If old value at i is needed later and i is now finalized to B_i, we may have lost one occurrence. If it was the last occurrence, then future positions needing that old value impossible. So before finalizing i, we should ensure the old value at i is not the last occurrence needed later, or duplicate it elsewhere. This is exactly multiset resource.

Maybe we can choose to finalize positions in decreasing order of "last needed" to preserve sources. For each value v, let last_B_position need v. We should not destroy the last occurrence of v before all positions needing v are finalized. If A_i = v and B_i != v, and i is the last remaining occurrence of v, then finalizing i would destroy v. But we could first copy v to some other position (buffer) within K, then finalize i. That buffer may be a position not yet finalized. So we can always duplicate a value if there is an unfinalized position within K to store it. This is like we need enough "space" to store values.

This suggests a greedy with "available values" in a sliding window. Since operation can copy any value within K, we can think of each value as being available at all positions within K of some occurrence. If we process final positions left to right, we can maintain a multiset of values that are "available" to be written to current position. But writing destroys the current value, which may affect availability.

It seems moving a unique value across a segment requires a buffer, and the buffer must be a duplicate of some value. This is like the 15-puzzle with indistinguishable tokens? There is a known concept: To transform one sequence to another by copying adjacent values, the "information" can flow but unique values act as obstacles.

Let's consider a different formalization: Since operation copies, the final value at each position is the initial value at some ancestor. The ancestry map f must be such that for each color c, the preimage f^{-1}(positions with initial c) maybe forms intervals? For K=1, f nondecreasing. For general K, maybe f must be "K-Lipschitz" in the sense that if f(i)=p, then |i-p| can be covered by a path that doesn't cross certain "barriers". But sample3 no maybe due to no such f with additional "no crossing" for K=1. Let's test sample3 with monotone ancestry map. Is there a nondecreasing f such that B_i = A_{f(i)}? Let's check.

But maybe there is a generalized condition: There exists a function f: final positions -> initial positions such that B_i = A_{f(i)} and f is "nondecreasing up to K"? More precisely, for any i<j, if f(i) > f(j), then the distance between f(i) and f(j) or the positions must allow crossing? Let's derive from path nonintersection.

In 1D with edges of length <=K, two lineages can cross without coalescing if at some time one jumps over the other. Suppose final positions i<j have ancestors f(i)=p > q=f(j) (inversion). To swap order, the path from i to p and j to q must cross. They can cross without meeting if there is a time when one lineage jumps from left of the other to right of the other in one step of length <=K. That requires the jump length (distance between the two lineages at that time) <=K. More generally, for any inversion, the distance between the two final positions? Let's look at the "coalescing paths" more formally. We have final positions (i, T) and initial positions (f(i), 0). We need to draw paths in the spacetime rectangle [1,N] x [0,T] that are continuous, move horizontally only along edges of length<=K (i.e., can jump from x to y if |x-y|<=K), and move forward in time. Paths cannot intersect except if they have same color? Actually if two paths intersect at same spacetime point, they coalesce and thereafter same, meaning their final colors would be same initial color. If their initial colors differ, they cannot intersect. If same color, intersection allowed. We can choose T large. This is exactly a problem of finding nonintersecting paths in a directed acyclic graph (DAG) with vertices (position, time). Since time is continuous/unbounded, we can use a topological argument: Such paths exist iff for every interval [l,r], the number of paths that need to cross from outside to inside satisfies something. This is like the "noncrossing matching" condition.

For K=1, paths move along adjacent edges; nonintersecting paths in a rectangle preserve order, so f must be nondecreasing. For K>1, paths can jump over each other if the jump spans over the other path without intersecting. In spacetime, a jump from x to y at time t is a horizontal segment at constant time. If another path has a point with position z between x and y at the same time t, the horizontal segment would cross it (intersect) if z lies on the segment. But if the other path is at a different time, no intersection. We can schedule jumps at different times to avoid. Since time unbounded, we can make paths cross by having one path jump over the other's position at a time when the other is not there? Wait the other path must be somewhere at every time between 0 and T. If it is at position z at time t, and our path jumps from x to y at time t, it will pass through z if z between x,y, causing intersection. We could choose a different time t' when the other path is not at z. But the other path exists at all times; it may move around. We can design its trajectory to be away from z at t'. Since time unbounded, we can coordinate. This is like we can route paths in 2D with obstacles that are other paths; but paths themselves can move. There is a known result: In a graph with edges of length<=K, the only obstruction is if K=1 and order? Let's test K=2, A=[1,2,3], B=[3,2,3] reachable yes. A=[1,2,3], B=[3,2,1]? Need reverse. We should never overwrite a matched position unless using it as temporary buffer and restore. Thus finalization process can be seen as: We have a set of mismatched positions. We can resolve a mismatch at i by copying B_i from some position j within K that currently has B_i. If j is mismatched and its current value is B_i, it remains mismatched (since its final value != B_i) but can be used as source; it will be resolved later. This reverse operation is a known "annihilation" process: We can change a position to any value if its current value appears again within distance K. Equivalently, we can "remove" an occurrence of value v if there is another v within K, and replace it with a "wildcard" that can be any value. We want to see if B can be reduced/transformed to A. Since we can set to any value, we can think of positions that are "flexible" once their current value is duplicated nearby.

This is promising! Let's formalize.

Forward operation: A_i <- A_j. Reverse operation: If current C_i = C_j = v and |i-j|<=K, then set C_i to any value u. (Because before forward, C_i could be u.) We can choose u arbitrarily, not necessarily from current values. This is a valid reverse step for some forward operation. Therefore, B is reachable from A iff A is reachable from B by these reverse operations? Need be careful: Reverse operation as defined allows changing i to any u, but in the actual forward sequence, u is the value at i before that forward operation, which is determined. If we can find a reverse sequence from B to A using arbitrary u, then reversing it gives a forward sequence from A to B? Each reverse step: from state C (after forward) with C_i=C_j=v, set C_i=u to get previous state D. In forward, from D, choose i,j, set D_i = D_j = v to get C. This requires D_j = v. In reverse step, we required C_j = v. Does D_j = v? Since reverse step only changes i, j remains v, so D_j = v. Good. So any reverse step corresponds to a valid forward step. Thus reachability is symmetric under this reverse operation? Wait forward operation can choose any source j with value v; reverse requires after state has i and j both v. In forward, after operation, both i and j are v (source j unchanged). So yes. Therefore, B reachable from A iff A reachable from B by reverse operations where we can set target to arbitrary value. But since arbitrary u, this is a different relation, not symmetric? Actually if B reachable from A, then reversing the exact sequence gives a reverse sequence from B to A with u being the old values. So reverse reachability is necessary. Is it sufficient? If there exists a reverse sequence from B to A using arbitrary u, then reversing it gives a forward sequence from A to B? Let's check: Reverse step from C to D: C_i=C_j=v, D_i=u, D_j=v. Forward step from D: choose i,j, set D_i = D_j = v, resulting C. This is valid because D_j=v. So yes, any reverse step is exactly invertible by a forward step. Thus if we can transform B to A by reverse operations, then reversing the sequence transforms A to B by forward operations. The arbitrary u in reverse is exactly the value that will be at i in the previous state; no constraint. So the reachability problem is equivalent to: Can we transform B into A using the operation: choose i,j with |i-j|<=K and C_i = C_j, then change C_i to any value? (We can also choose i=j? no change). This is much easier because the target value is arbitrary.

Let's analyze reverse operation more abstractly. We have current colors C. A position i is "removable" (can be changed) if there exists j with |i-j|<=K and C_i=C_j. When we remove/change i, we can set it to any target value. We want to reach D. Actually after changing a position to some value, it may need to be changed again. But we can always change a position again if it is free. The final value D_i can be set on the last change of i. So the goal is to ensure every position can be changed at least once (if needed) and then set to D_i.

Maybe we can model "locked" positions as tokens that need to be "activated" by a free position of the same color within K. Once activated, they become free and can be changed. Changing a free position from color v to something else may deactivate other positions of color v. This is exactly a game of "lights out" on a graph where colors are tokens.

Since we can set changed positions to arbitrary colors, we can always use a free position as a "wildcard" to create a witness for any locked position. But doing so consumes the free position's current color, potentially deactivating others.

This is similar to the "15-puzzle" with indistinguishable tokens: Free positions are like empty spaces? Let's map.

In reverse, a color v occurrence can be deleted if there is another v within K. This is like we have tokens of colors; a token can be removed if it is not the only token of its color in its K-neighborhood. We can also create arbitrary tokens at removed positions. To reach D, we need remove tokens that are in B but not in A, and create tokens that are in A but not in B. The spatial constraint is about removal: a token can be removed only if it has a neighbor token of same color within K. This is like "annihilation" of duplicate tokens. If a color appears only once in B and that occurrence is not needed in A, we need to remove it, but it has no duplicate, so impossible unless we first create another token of that color at a nearby position (by changing some other removable token to that color). Creating a token of color v requires removing some other color u, which must have a duplicate. Thus we need a "chain" of removals.

This is exactly the concept of "pebble game" or "graph searching with colors" where each color has a count. Maybe we can solve by considering counts and connected components of positions where B_i != A_i?

Maybe we can think of the reverse process as: We have a multiset of "available witnesses" (colors with at least two occurrences within K). We can use a witness of color v to change a position to any color u. This consumes one occurrence of v (the changed position) and creates one occurrence of u. Thus the multiset counts change: count_v--, count_u++. This is exactly the same as forward multiset reaction, but with the additional constraint that the position changed must be within K of another v. In terms of counts, any color with count>=2 can be decreased; any color can be increased. So multiset reachability in reverse is: Can we transform counts of B to counts of A by repeatedly decrementing a color with count>=2 and incrementing any color? This is easy: We can decrease any color that has count>=2, and increase any color arbitrarily. Since increasing doesn't require the color to exist. Therefore, as long as there is at least one color with count>=2 initially (or created), we can adjust counts to any target counts? Let's check B=[1,2,2] counts {1:1,2:2}, target A=[2,2,1] counts {1:1,2:2} same. Counts already match, but spatial impossible. So counts not enough.

But the reverse operation also has spatial constraint: to decrement color v, the changed position must be within K of another v. This means we can only delete occurrences that are in a K-connected component of size>=2. The position where we increment u can be anywhere? The changed position is the deleted v occurrence; so u is created at that same position. Thus we can move "mass" of colors through positions.

This is analogous to a "token" of color v at position i can be changed to u if there is another v within K. So the new u appears at i. This is like a token of v is "annihilated" with a neighboring v to produce u at i. The neighboring v remains. Thus a pair of adjacent v's can act as a "factory" to produce any color at one of the pair's positions. After production, the pair may break.

Maybe we can solve using graph search on positions with DSU of "active" positions. Since values can be arbitrary, perhaps the only thing that matters is whether each position can be made "active" at some point. In a successful reverse sequence, every position that needs to change (B_i != A_i) must be changed at least once. This is exactly the problem of "graph searching with tokens" known as "graph pebbling" maybe solvable by greedy: process positions in order of target A; maintain a set of "available" positions that can be changed. Initially available if B has duplicate within K. When we change an available position i to A_i (final), it may make other positions of color A_i available if they are within K. But if we set i to A_i and then never change it, it becomes a permanent witness for A_i. This is like the forward one-change process but in reverse! Let's see.

Reverse process: We want to transform B to A. We can process positions one by one, changing them to their target A_i and then never changing them again. Is this always possible if a sequence exists? Maybe not, because a position might need to be changed to a non-target value temporarily to serve as witness, then changed again. But in reverse, if we change a position to its target early, it can serve as a permanent witness for that target color, which seems helpful. Could it hurt? It might destroy a witness for its old color needed to change other positions. But if we process in the right order, we can avoid. This is exactly the same as forward one-change but with arbitrary target? Actually reverse operation changes a position from current color to any value; if we set it to target A_i, it becomes fixed. We need ensure that at the time of change, its current color has a witness within K. This is analogous to the original forward problem with roles of A and B swapped and target arbitrary? Let's formalize:

Reverse one-change algorithm:
- Positions not yet processed have their current value from B (or intermediate? but we won't change them until processed).
- Processed positions are fixed to A_i.
- At each step, choose an unprocessed position i whose current value v appears at another unprocessed position j within K OR at a processed position? Wait if the witness is processed, its value is A_j, not necessarily v. The witness must have current value v at the time. Processed positions have value A_j, so they can witness only if A_j = v. Unprocessed positions have their original B value (if not changed yet). Since we only change each position once, unprocessed positions still have B_i. Thus a position i can be processed if B_i appears at some other position j within K that is either unprocessed with B_j = B_i, or processed with A_j = B_i. After processing i, it becomes A_i and can witness for positions needing A_i.

This is exactly the forward graph search but with B as initial and A as target, and the operation is "set i to A_i" if B_i is available within K. Wait forward operation set i to B_i using source with B_i; here reverse sets i to A_i using source with B_i. The source has the old value, not the target. This is like we are "removing" the old value.

Is every reachable reverse sequence reducible to one-change in this manner? Suppose a position is changed multiple times in reverse. The last change sets it to A_i. Before that, it may have been changed to intermediate values to serve as witnesses. If we instead leave it at B_i until its last change, could it still serve as a witness for B_i? It would have B_i, which is its original value, so yes it can witness B_i. But if it was changed to some intermediate u to witness u, leaving it at B_i would not witness u. However maybe another position can witness u. Not guaranteed. So one-change may not be complete. But perhaps for this problem, due to arbitrary target, one-change is complete? This one-change reverse process is promising. It is essentially: We have initial colors B. We want to recolor each position to A_i exactly once, in some order. At the time we recolor i, its current color (which is B_i if not recolored yet) must be present at some position j within K that is either not yet recolored and has B_j = B_i, or already recolored and has A_j = B_i. Because if j already recolored, its current color is A_j; if not, B_j. Since we recolor each once, current color of unprocessed is B_i. This is a deterministic state: processed positions have A, unprocessed have B. We need find an order to process all positions (or at least all mismatched; positions with A_i=B_i can be considered already processed initially) such that each position i, when processed, has a witness for B_i within K among processed/unprocessed positions.

This is exactly a graph search problem! Let's define:
- Initially, processed set P = {i | A_i = B_i} (they already match, no need to change; they can serve as witnesses with color A_i = B_i).
- Unprocessed positions have color B_i.
- We can process an unprocessed position i if there exists j with |i-j|<=K such that:
   - if j is unprocessed, B_j = B_i (and j != i)
   - if j is processed, A_j = B_i.
After processing i, it becomes processed with color A_i.

We need process all positions (or all mismatched; matched already processed).

This is much simpler and likely the intended solution! Let's test with examples and see if it matches reachability. Is there a case where one-change process fails but multi-change reverse succeeds? Let's think. Reverse operation can change a position multiple times. In one-change, once processed, its color is A_i. If A_i is not some needed witness, it's lost. What about B=[1,2,3,2], A=[2,3,2,1], K=1? Let's not.

I suspect the one-change reverse process is complete. Let's try to prove: Given any reverse sequence from B to A, consider the last time each position is changed. Since final is A, the last change of position i sets it to A_i. Before that last change, the position may have been changed multiple times. We can modify the sequence to omit all earlier changes of i, leaving it at B_i until its last change. Would the last change still be valid? At the last change of i, its current value in the original sequence is some value v (which may not be B_i if earlier changes occurred). To set it to A_i, the reverse operation requires a witness j with current value v within K. If we omit earlier changes of i, at that time i would have B_i instead of v, so the operation would not be valid (we need witness for B_i, not v). However, maybe we can instead process i at the very beginning? Not clear.

But perhaps any successful reverse sequence can be transformed into one where each position is changed at most once by "commuting" operations. Since reverse operation changes a position to arbitrary value, if a position is changed multiple times, the intermediate changes only serve to provide witnesses. We could replace the intermediate value at i by the value it is supposed to witness? Hmm.

Maybe any reachable can be done with each position changed at most once! Let's try to prove: Given a forward sequence, take the last operation on each position. We can reorder operations so that each position's last operation occurs in increasing order of time? But if a position p is used as source for value v at some time, and later p changes to final, then p's value v at that time must have been obtained either initially or by an earlier operation on p. If v is not p's final, then p's last operation changes it away from v, so p was an intermediate. Could we instead use the final position of v that will be created later? Not necessarily.

Let's attempt a proof of one-change completeness for reverse process. Given any successful reverse sequence from B to A. Consider the first time a position is changed? Actually we want to show there is a sequence where each position changed at most once. This is equivalent to the existence of an ordering of positions such that each position i, when processed, has B_i present within K among positions not yet processed or positions already processed with A_j = B_i. This is a known property of "graph searching" with "irreversible" conversions. Is every reachable state in the reversible (multi-change) process also reachable in the irreversible one-change process? Not generally. But maybe for this specific operation with arbitrary target, yes due to "monotonicity": The set of positions that can be changed to their final values expands monotonically if we never change a position away from final. If a position is changed multiple times in a successful sequence, consider the last change of each position. Let P be the set of positions that have already had their last change (i.e., are final). At the moment of the last change of position i, its current value v is witnessed by some j. If j has not yet had its last change, then j's current value is not necessarily B_j (it may have been changed before). But we could instead process i earlier, before j's previous changes, when j's value was B_j? Not if v was created by j's previous changes. However, maybe we can use the fact that v must be B_i? Wait in reverse, the last change sets i to A_i, not necessarily from B_i. The value v being changed away is the current value before last change. In a one-change process, we would change i from B_i to A_i. If v != B_i, then i was changed earlier to v. That earlier change created v at i. Could we omit that earlier change and instead change i directly from B_i to A_i at the time of the last change? The witness for the last change is some j with value v. If we omit earlier changes, i has B_i, so we need a witness for B_i, not v. But maybe we can process i at the time of its first change instead? At first change, i's value is B_i, and there is a witness for B_i. If we set i directly to A_i at that time, would the operation be valid? Reverse operation allows changing i to any value, so yes! We don't need to change i to intermediate v first. If we set i to A_i at the time of its first change, then i becomes final earlier. This might affect witnesses for other positions that relied on i having intermediate v. But if we process i later instead? Hmm.

Suppose a position i is changed first from B_i to v (intermediate), later from v to A_i. At the first change, there is a witness for B_i. We could instead at that same time change i to A_i (valid, since target arbitrary). Then i is final. Later, when original sequence needed i to have v as witness for another position k, i would have A_i instead of v. But perhaps k could use the witness that was used to create v at i? Not sure.

This is like we can "shortcut" a position's changes to its final value at its first change, but this may break the chain. However, if we process positions in the order of their first changes, maybe each first change can be redirected to final, and the intermediate values are not needed because the witnesses for those intermediate values are the positions that would have been changed before? Let's test with a hypothetical chain: B has 1 at i, 2 at j. To create 2 at i (intermediate), need witness 2 at j. Then use i as witness to change k from2 to A_k. If we instead set i to A_i at first change, i might not be2, so k cannot change. But maybe we can change k before i? k needs witness2; j has2, if j within K of k, yes. If not, i was bridging. So intermediate i may be necessary to propagate 2 across a gap. This is exactly moving a value across a region. In such a case, is the overall transformation reachable? It might be, if there is enough buffer. One-change process would fail because k's B_k=2 has no witness within K initially. But maybe the transformation is still reachable with intermediates. Let's try to construct a reachable example requiring propagation of a value across a gap, with enough buffer, and see if one-change fails.

Consider K=1. We want to move value 2 from right to left across a gap, using intermediate positions. Let B (reverse start) have a 2 at far right, and target A wants some position left to be2, while intermediate positions final not2. For reverse, to change a left position from2 to something? Wait reverse changes B to A. If A has 2 at left and B doesn't, we need create 2 at left. Reverse operation can create 2 at a position by changing some other value to2, but to change that position, its current value must have a witness. To create 2 at left, we need a position left that is changeable. If no changeable position near left, we need propagate changeability from right to left by changing positions to intermediate values. This is like a wave of changeability. One-change process would require the left position's B value to have a witness, not the target 2. It might fail if the left position's B value is unique.

Example: B = [1,2,3]? Let's design reverse B to A where A wants 2 at pos1, B pos1=1 unique, pos2=2, pos3=3. K=1. To change pos1 from1 to2, need witness1. None. Could change pos2 from2 to1 (witness? pos2=2 no other2) no. So no changeable positions initially. Need B have some duplicate to start. Add duplicate: B=[1,2,2,3], A=[2,2,3,1]? We want create 2 at pos1, move 1 to pos4. This is similar to earlier B=[1,2,2] to A=[2,2,1] unreachable. Add more buffer: B=[1,2,2,2,3], A=[2,2,2,3,1]? Move 1 from pos1 to pos5, 2s left, 3 to pos4. Is it reachable with K=1? Let's try reverse one-change:
B: 1 2 2 2 3. A: 2 2 2 3 1.

Let's not rely on proof yet; let's see if the one-change process can be solved efficiently and matches samples. We'll later try to justify.

One-change reverse process formalized:
- We have arrays B (initial for reverse) and A (target).
- A position i is "fixed" if A_i = B_i initially, or after we process it.
- We need process all positions. At any time, for an unprocessed position i, its current value is B_i. It can be processed if there exists j with |i-j|<=K such that:
   * j is unprocessed and B_j = B_i, or
   * j is fixed and A_j = B_i.
After processing, i becomes fixed with value A_i.

This is equivalent to a graph reachability problem on positions where each position has two "colors": B_i (while unprocessed) and A_i (after processed). We need find an ordering.

This looks like a 2-SAT? Or can be solved greedily with queues? At any step, if an unprocessed position i is processable, we can process it. Does processing a position ever make another processable position unprocessable? Processing i changes its color from B_i to A_i. This can affect processability of other positions:
- For another unprocessed position k with B_k = B_i, i was a witness for k (if within K). After processing, i's color becomes A_i, so if A_i != B_k, k loses that witness. Thus processing i can make k unprocessable! This is the key difficulty. In graph search, processing a node can disable others. So greedy queue may fail (as seen in examples where processing wrong order deadlocks). Need find an ordering, not just closure.

This is analogous to "graph searching with monotone decreasing resources" and may be solvable by considering the last processed position? Let's analyze.

We need process all positions. Consider the last position processed in the order. Just before processing it, all other positions are fixed (processed), and it is the only unprocessed. Its current value is B_i. It can be processed if there exists j within K with A_j = B_i (since all other positions fixed). Thus for the last position i, we need a fixed position j (could be any other) with A_j = B_i within K. This is a necessary condition. After processing i, it becomes A_i.

This suggests a backward elimination process: Instead of processing from B to A, consider the reverse of the processing order. If we reverse the one-change process, we start with all positions fixed to A, and "unprocess" positions one by one, changing them from A_i back to B_i. The reverse of processing i (which changed B_i to A_i) is: choose an unprocessed? Let's define:
Forward one-change: unprocessed have B, processed have A. Processing i requires a witness for B_i among processed (A) or unprocessed (B).
If we reverse the order, we start with all positions processed (A). We want to "unprocess" positions until all are unprocessed (B). Unprocessing i changes A_i to B_i. What is the condition? In the forward order, when i was processed, there was a witness for B_i. In the reversed order, at the moment we unprocess i (which is before i was processed in forward), the witness for B_i must still be "available": it could be a position that is still processed (A_j = B_i) or already unprocessed (B_j = B_i). But if we reverse, processed set shrinks. This is similar but maybe easier? The last processed in forward is the first unprocessed in reverse. Condition for first unprocessed i: there exists j with A_j = B_i within K (since all processed initially). This is same as above. After unprocessing i, it becomes B_i and can serve as witness for B_i for later unprocessing. We need unprocess all positions. This is exactly the same problem with roles? It's symmetric: Start with all A, need convert to B by unprocessing; condition for unprocessing i: A_i? Wait unprocessing changes A_i to B_i. To be valid, we need a witness for B_i (the value we are restoring), not for A_i. The witness can be a processed position with A_j = B_i or an unprocessed position with B_j = B_i. This is identical to the original forward process but with initial "processed" set all positions? Actually original forward one-change starts with some positions processed (where A=B) and unprocessed B. The reverse starts with all processed A and unprocesses all. This might be easier because initially all positions are processed, so any position i with a neighbor j such that A_j = B_i can be unprocessed. After unprocessing, it becomes B_i and can help unprocess others needing B_i.

This is like we have two arrays A and B. We need find an order to "switch" each position from A_i to B_i (unprocess) such that when switching i, B_i is present within K either at a position still A (A_j=B_i) or already switched (B_j=B_i). This is exactly the same as the forward one-change but with A and B swapped? Forward one-change processed B->A requiring B_i witness. Reverse unprocess A->B requiring B_i witness. Both require witness for B_i. The set of positions already switched have B, not switched have A. Initially none switched. We need switch all. This is a monotone process: switching a position from A_i to B_i adds a witness for B_i and removes a potential witness for A_i? Wait it removes A_i as a witness for positions needing A_i? The condition for switching i requires witness for B_i, not A_i. So switching i does not need A_i. But after switching, A_i is no longer present at i; this could affect the ability to switch other positions? Other positions' condition depends on B_k, not A_i. They may have used A_i as a witness for B_k if A_i = B_k. If we switch i away from A_i, and A_i = B_k, then k loses a witness. So switching can disable others. This is the same issue.

But maybe we can choose the last switch (in reverse) instead. Let's think of the forward one-change process as "we need to eliminate all B_i values by replacing with A_i, but B_i must have a witness until eliminated." This is like we have a set of "tokens" B_i at unprocessed positions. Processing i removes the token B_i at i and creates token A_i at i. The condition to remove token B_i is that there is another token B_i within K (either still unprocessed, or already processed? processed positions have A_j, not B_j; but if A_j = B_i, then it's a token B_i too). Actually after processing, the color at j is A_j; if A_j = B_i, then it is a token of value B_i. So at any time, the multiset of colors present is: for processed positions, A_j; for unprocessed, B_j. We need remove B_i tokens and add A_i tokens. This is exactly the original forward multiset reaction but with each position's target.

This is a known problem of "graph searching with tokens" that can be solved by considering "pebble game" and using maximum matching? Maybe.

The one-change process condition might be equivalent to: For every value x, the positions where B_i = x and A_i != x must be "matched" to positions with A_j = x or B_j = x within K, forming a directed graph; need acyclic? This is like we have a directed graph from each position i (needing to change from B_i to A_i) to a witness j with value B_i. If j is unprocessed, it must be processed after i? Wait if witness j is unprocessed with B_j = B_i, then j still has B_i. We can process i using j as witness; j will be processed later. Thus dependency edge i -> j where j processed after i. If witness j is processed with A_j = B_i, then j processed before i. So dependencies can go forward or backward in time depending on whether witness's current value is B_j or A_j. This is exactly the mixed dependency graph we saw earlier. Need find an ordering satisfying these "before/after" constraints. This is a 2-SAT-like problem? Each position i chooses a witness j. If B_j = B_i (j unprocessed at that time), then j must be after i. If A_j = B_i (j processed), then j must be before i. We need choose witnesses and an order. This is like finding a feedback-free orientation in a graph of possible witnesses. Maybe there is a simpler characterization: The process is possible iff there is no "closed" set S of positions such that for every i in S, all witnesses for B_i are also in S and have B_j = B_i? Let's derive.

Consider the first position processed in the forward one-change order. At that time, no positions have been processed yet (except initial matches). Actually initial matches are already processed. Let P0 = {i | A_i = B_i}. The first newly processed position i must have a witness j with B_j = B_i within K, where j is unprocessed (since no newly processed yet; initial processed could also witness if A_j = B_i, but if A_j=B_i then j in P0). After processing some set S, the next position i can be processed if B_i has a witness in (unprocessed positions with B=B_i) ∪ (processed positions with A=B_i). This is equivalent to: In the subgraph of positions not yet processed, consider the value B_i. If the connected component (via edges where both endpoints have same B value?) of i among unprocessed positions has size >=2, then i has an unprocessed witness. Or if there is a processed position j with A_j = B_i within K, then also.

Thus a position i becomes processable when either:
- It has a neighbor j (within K) that is unprocessed and B_j = B_i.
- It has a neighbor j that is processed and A_j = B_i.

Processing i removes it from the unprocessed B_i-component, which may cause other positions in that component to lose their unprocessed witness. But they might become processable via processed witnesses.

This is similar to "graph contraction" where we delete vertices one by one; a vertex can be deleted if it has a neighbor of the same "current color" (either B if unprocessed, or A if processed). We want delete all vertices, changing their color from B to A. This is exactly the "graph searching" problem known as "graph pebbling" or "graph burning" with two states.

Maybe we can solve it with a greedy algorithm using a priority queue of positions that are currently processable. When processing a position, it may make other positions unprocessable (if they lost their only same-B unprocessed neighbor), but it may also make positions processable (if A_i equals their B_i). The non-monotonicity means a simple queue can fail if we process a position that disables others. But perhaps we can always process a processable position that is "safe" (e.g., one whose B_i value has another witness that won't be disabled). Need find if any order exists.

This is reminiscent of "topological sorting with AND/OR" and can be solved by checking if there is a "kernel" or using maximum matching in bipartite graphs. Let's search memory of ARC124D editorial. We need find a permutation such that each i has a predecessor/successor. This is equivalent to finding a Hamiltonian path in a directed graph? But N large, so there must be a simpler condition.

Could there be an algorithm based on "peeling" processable positions, but with backtracking? Since processing can disable others, we need choose order. However, maybe if a position is processable, processing it never disables a position that was not already processable? Let's check. Processing i changes color from B_i to A_i. It can disable an unprocessed position k if k's only witness for B_k was i (with B_i = B_k) and k is not otherwise processable. So k was processable before (via i). Thus processing can disable processable positions. But if we process i, k might become unprocessable. In a valid order, we must process k before i if i is its only witness. This is a dependency: k must be before i. This can be represented as constraints.

For each pair of positions i,k with |i-k|<=K and B_i = B_k, they can witness each other while both unprocessed. If we process one before the other, the later can use the earlier? Wait if i processed before k, i's color becomes A_i. If A_i != B_k, then i no longer witnesses k. So k cannot use i after i is processed. Thus if k needs i as witness, k must be processed before i. If A_i = B_k, then i remains a witness after processing, so k can be after i. Similarly, a processed position j with A_j = B_i can witness i if j is processed before i. So for each potential witness j for i:
- If B_j = B_i and A_j != B_i, then j must be processed after i (otherwise j's B witness disappears before i).
- If B_j = B_i and A_j = B_i, then j can be before or after (it always has B_i).
- If A_j = B_i (and j processed), then j must be processed before i (to have A_j). Note if B_j = B_i and A_j = B_i, both conditions.

Thus each position i needs at least one witness j satisfying the timing constraints. This is like a system of "before/after" constraints. We can choose witnesses. This is similar to finding a topological ordering with "OR" constraints. Could be NP-hard in general? But on a path with interval constraints maybe polynomial.

Let's see if B=[1,1,2], A=[2,1,2] (pos2 matched) K=1. Process pos1 B=1 witness pos2 processed? pos2 A=1=B2, so processed initially. Set pos1=2 -> [2,1,2]. Works. Condition holds.

Now, how to solve the one-change process efficiently? It resembles "graph searching" with two colors per vertex. Maybe there is a known algorithm using "maximum matching" or "2-SAT" but perhaps the graph is an interval graph and can be solved greedily.

Let's formulate as a dependency graph. For each position i, define:
- Need to process i (if A_i != B_i; if equal, already processed).
- To process i, choose a witness j within K such that either:
   a) j is processed before i and A_j = B_i.
   b) j is processed after i and B_j = B_i. (If j initially processed, it's before; if j unprocessed and B_j=B_i, we can choose after.)
If B_j = B_i and A_j = B_i (i.e., A_j=B_j=B_i), then j is initially processed and also before; okay.

This is like each position i must have an incoming edge from a witness that is ordered appropriately. We can think of constructing the processing order from last to first. Let's reverse the order (unprocessing from A to B). In reverse, initially all positions are processed (A). We unprocess positions one by one. When unprocessing i, we change A_i to B_i. The condition for unprocessing i is that B_i is present within K at that time, either at a position still processed (A_j = B_i) or already unprocessed (B_j = B_i). This is exactly the same as forward but starting from all A and ending at all B. This symmetry might allow a greedy algorithm: In reverse, the first position to unprocess is the last processed in forward. It must have a neighbor j with A_j = B_i (since no unprocessed yet). After unprocessing i, it becomes B_i and can help unprocess others. This is monotone in the sense that unprocessing adds B_i tokens and removes A_i tokens. It can disable positions that needed A_i as witness for their B_k? Wait unprocessing i removes A_i. A position k (still processed) will later be unprocessed; its condition requires B_k present. Consider the forward process. At any time, processed set P. A position i not in P is processable if B_i ∈ N_P(i) ∪ N_U(i) where N_P(i) = {A_j : j∈P, |i-j|<=K}, N_U(i) = {B_j : j∉P, j≠i, |i-j|<=K}. If a set S of unprocessed positions has no processable position, then for every i∈S, B_i does not appear within K outside S? More precisely, all positions j within K with current color B_i are either in S (unprocessed with B_j=B_i) or processed with A_j=B_i. If none, stuck. Maybe the one-change reverse process can be solved using a known algorithm: "graph reachability with two states" is equivalent to checking if there is an ordering such that each vertex has an incoming edge from a vertex earlier or later. Let's model the one-change process as a directed graph of dependencies. For each position i, define possible witnesses W_i = {j != i : |i-j|<=K and (B_j = B_i or A_j = B_i)}. If j is initially processed (A_j=B_j=B_i), then j can be before i. If j is unprocessed and B_j=B_i, then j can be after i (or before if A_j=B_i too). We need choose for each i a witness j and an order such that:
- If B_j = B_i and A_j != B_i, then j must be after i.
- If A_j = B_i and B_j != B_i, then j must be before i.
- If both, no constraint (j can be before or after).
- If neither, j not a witness.

This is like each position i needs at least one "incoming" edge from a witness that is ordered appropriately. We can think of constructing the order from left to right? Maybe because graph is an interval graph, the order of processing can be assumed to be monotone by position? Not necessarily; sample2 order was pos2, pos4, pos3, pos5? Forward one-change order for sample2 reverse? Let's find one-change order for reverse B->A sample2:

Now pos4 B=2,A=1, witness pos1 processed A=2 -> process.

This constraint satisfaction might be solvable by 2-SAT? Each position chooses a witness among a set; constraints are ordering between i and chosen j. But we can choose witnesses to avoid cycles. This is like finding a topological ordering of a directed graph where each vertex needs an incoming edge from a predecessor or successor. Actually if we fix an order, for each position i, the witness must be either before (if A_j=B_i) or after (if B_j=B_i). This is equivalent to: In the order, for each i, there is a position j within K such that either j is before i and A_j=B_i, or j is after i and B_j=B_i. This is a "betweenness" condition.

Maybe we can determine feasibility by considering the last position in the order. The last position i must have a witness j before it with A_j = B_i (since no after). Thus there must exist j with A_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K. After removing i, the problem reduces to the same on remaining positions? Because if i is last, it doesn't need to witness for anyone after (none), but it could have been a witness for others before? In forward order, if i is last, it is unprocessed until the end, so it can serve as a witness for B_i for positions processed before it (since B_j = B_i). Thus removing i from the set of unprocessed positions might affect earlier positions' witnesses. However, if we build the order backwards (unprocessing), the first unprocessed position in reverse is the last processed in forward. The condition for it is just A_j = B_i within K (since all others processed). After unprocessing it, it becomes B_i and can serve as a witness for B_i for later unprocessing. This is a monotone process in reverse? Unprocessing a position adds a B_i token and removes an A_i token. It can disable positions that needed A_i as witness for their B_k, but those positions are still processed and will be unprocessed later; they might have other witnesses. This is exactly the same problem but maybe easier to solve greedily by repeatedly finding a position i that can be unprocessed (has A_j = B_i within K among processed positions OR B_j = B_i among already unprocessed positions). Initially no unprocessed, so first condition only. After unprocessing some, second condition also. This is like we start with all positions "active" as A, and we want to deactivate them to B. A position can be deactivated if B_i is present within K either at an active position with A_j=B_i or at an inactive position with B_j=B_i. This is symmetric to forward but with initial active set all.

This is similar to "graph searching" and can be solved by a greedy algorithm using a queue, because deactivating a position (changing A_i to B_i) might make other positions undeactivatable if they relied on A_i. But if we process in reverse order (last to first), the first deactivated is the last forward; after deactivating, it can only help (adds B_i) and can hurt (removes A_i). The hurt is only for positions k that need A_i = B_k. But if k needs A_i, then i must be deactivated after k in forward? Let's see: In forward, k processed before i if k uses A_i as witness (i processed before k? Wait A_i is available only after i is processed. So if k uses A_i = B_k, then i must be processed before k. Thus in forward order, i before k. In reverse deactivation order (which is reverse of forward), k is deactivated before i. So if we deactivate i first in reverse, we violate k's need. Therefore, in reverse, we should deactivate k before i. This is a dependency: k before i in reverse if A_i = B_k. This is the same issue.

This is a classic problem of "finding an elimination ordering in a directed graph" which can be solved by topological sorting if we can orient edges. For each pair (i,k) with |i-k|<=K and A_i = B_k, if k uses i as witness, then forward i before k, reverse k before i. But k might have other witnesses.

Maybe we can formulate as a bipartite matching between positions and witnesses, and then check if the directed graph has a cycle. Since each position needs one witness, and witnesses impose order. pos1 uses pos2 (B_j=1) -> pos2 after pos1.

Let's derive from the dependency graph more rigorously.

In the one-change process, each position i (with A_i != B_i) must choose a witness j. The witness relation can be:
- Type P (processed before): j is processed before i, and A_j = B_i.
- Type U (unprocessed after): j is unprocessed after i, and B_j = B_i.
If j is initially processed (A_j=B_j), it can be type P. If j is unprocessed and A_j=B_i, it cannot be type U unless B_j=B_i too.

We can think of the process order as a permutation π. For each i, there must exist j such that:
   (π(j) < π(i) and A_j = B_i) OR (π(j) > π(i) and B_j = B_i).
This is a "betweenness" condition. This is equivalent to: For each i, B_i appears in the set of colors of positions before i (with A) or after i (with B). This is exactly the definition of a "perfect elimination ordering" for a bipartite graph between positions and colors. Maybe we can find such an ordering by repeatedly removing a position i that has B_i appearing in the remaining set? Let's see.

If we build the order from last to first (reverse), when we remove the last position i, all other positions are before it, so condition for i is: there exists j before i with A_j = B_i. After removing i, for the remaining positions, the condition is the same but with the set of positions reduced; however, positions removed (after them) can still serve as type U witnesses? In reverse construction, removed positions are after, so they can serve as type U (B_j=B_i) for remaining positions. Thus when we remove a position i from the end, we should add B_i to the set of "available after" colors for the remaining positions. Initially, no after positions. We need remove positions one by one; a position i can be removed (made last among remaining) if either:
- There exists a remaining position j with A_j = B_i (before witness), OR
- There exists an already removed position j with B_j = B_i (after witness).
Because if j is already removed, it is after i in the final order, so it can be type U. This is exactly the reverse unprocessing process! Wait this is the same as before but now we build from last to first:
- Start with all positions unremoved. Removed set R = empty (these will be after).
- At each step, choose an unremoved position i to be the next last among remaining. It can be removed if there exists j != i such that:
   * j is still unremoved and A_j = B_i (j will be before i), OR
   * j is already removed and B_j = B_i (j will be after i).
After removing i, it becomes an after-position with B_i available.
This is a monotone process? Removing i adds B_i to after-set and removes A_i from before-set. It can disable positions that needed A_i as before-witness. But those positions are still unremoved and might need A_i; if so, they should have been removed before i. This is the same issue, but now the condition for removal uses A_j for unremoved and B_j for removed. This is symmetric to forward but maybe easier because we can choose the last position first. This is exactly the "unprocessing" process described earlier, starting from all A and ending at all B? Wait here we start with all positions unremoved (not processed), and remove them to build order from last to first. The condition for removal uses A_j for unremoved (before) and B_j for removed (after). This is the same as the forward one-change process but with A and B swapped? Let's compare:
Forward process: unprocessed have B, processed have A. Process i if B_i present among unprocessed (B) or processed (A).
Backward removal: unremoved have A, removed have B. Remove i if B_i present among unremoved (A) or removed (B).
So it's the same process with A and B swapped! Indeed, if we swap A and B, forward process becomes: unprocessed have A, processed have B; process i if A_i present among unprocessed (A) or processed (B). Not exactly. But backward removal condition is: B_i present among unremoved A or removed B. This is like forward process where target is B and initial is A? Let's define forward process from A to B: unprocessed have A, processed have B. Process i if A_i? No, forward operation sets i to B_i using source with B_i? Wait original forward operation sets A_i to B_i (target) using source with B_i. In one-change forward from A to B, unprocessed have A, processed have B. To process i, need B_i present among unprocessed (A_j = B_i) or processed (B_j = B_i). This is exactly the backward removal condition! So the backward removal from B to A is equivalent to forward one-change from A to B. Thus the one-change reverse process from B to A is equivalent to the one-change forward process from A to B. That makes sense: reverse of reverse.

So the problem reduces to: Given a directed graph H where for each mismatched position i, there are possible edges:
- For each j within K with A_j = B_i and B_j != B_i: edge i -> j (i before j).

We need choose for each mismatched i at least one incident edge (incoming or outgoing) such that the chosen directed graph is acyclic. If acyclic, topological order works. If there is a cycle, impossible.

This is exactly the problem of finding an acyclic orientation with each vertex having at least one incident edge. But note edges are already directed; we choose a subset. This is equivalent to: Is there a topological ordering of all vertices such that for each mismatched i, there is an edge from i to a later vertex (outgoing) or from an earlier vertex to i (incoming)? In other words, in the ordering, each mismatched vertex must have at least one "witness" edge to a vertex on the correct side. This is the original condition.

Can we find such an ordering by a simple greedy algorithm? Yes! Can we solve this removal process greedily? It is analogous to topological sorting with "available" set. A vertex i is removable if B_i is in the set of colors of remaining vertices (with B) or removed vertices (with A). Removing i changes these sets. This is like we have two multisets: Remaining B-colors and Removed A-colors. A vertex i can be removed if its B_i is present in either multiset (excluding itself). After removal, we remove B_i from remaining multiset and add A_i to removed multiset. We need remove all vertices. This is a much cleaner formulation!

So the removal process:
- Let U be set of mismatched positions (A_i != B_i). Matched positions are "fixed" and remain as before-witnesses with B value.
- Maintain multiset R_B of B values of all positions not yet removed (including matched and unmatched remaining).
- Maintain multiset D_A of A values of removed positions (these are after-witnesses).
- Initially R_B contains B_i for all positions; D_A empty.
- We can remove an unmatched position i if B_i appears in R_B at least twice? Because one occurrence is i itself; need another position j with B_j=B_i. Or B_i appears in D_A (from a removed position with A_j=B_i). More precisely, count_R(B_i) >= 2 OR count_D(B_i) >= 1. (If i matched, no need remove.)
- When we remove i, we decrement count_R(B_i) (remove its B from remaining) and increment count_D(A_i) (add its A to removed after-witnesses).
We need remove all unmatched positions. This is a much simpler multiset process! Does it capture distance K? We forgot distance! The witness must be within K, not just anywhere. We need maintain counts in a sliding window? The condition for i: there exists j within distance K with B_j=B_i (remaining) or A_j=B_i (removed). - There is another remaining position j within K with B_j = B_i.
- Or there is a removed position j within K with A_j = B_i.
When we remove i, we update local counts for positions within K. This is like a graph elimination process. This might be solvable with a priority queue and DSU? Since each removal only affects O(K) neighbors, but K can be large. Need efficient.

But maybe the distance condition can be simplified by considering the "nearest" occurrence. For each value v, the positions with B_i=v form clusters. A position i can be removed via a before-witness if it is not the only remaining B_i in its K-neighborhood. This is equivalent to: in the set of remaining positions with B_i=v, i has a neighbor within K. If we remove positions, clusters shrink. This is similar to "peeling" clusters of same B value. The after-witness D_A provides additional witnesses.

This looks like a graph problem on N vertices with edges between positions within K. We have two colors per vertex: B (while remaining) and A (after removed). We need eliminate all mismatched vertices; a vertex can be eliminated if its B color appears on a neighbor (remaining B or removed A). This is exactly the same as the original one-change but now we have a clean elimination rule. Is there a known algorithm? It is a "graph searching" process that is monotone? Removing a vertex can only decrease remaining B counts and increase removed A counts. It can make other vertices unremovable (if they relied on the removed B) or removable (if A_i matches their B). This is not monotone, but the state is fully described by remaining B counts and removed A counts.

Maybe we can solve it by considering the last removed vertex. The last removed vertex i must have B_i present among matched positions (never removed) or among? But D_A contains all removed positions; if some removed j has A_j=B_i, then j was removed before i and can serve as after-witness. This is recursive.

This is reminiscent of "graph coloring" and can be solved by checking if there is an ordering such that each vertex has a neighbor of color B_i in the set of vertices not yet removed (with B) or already removed (with A). This is exactly the definition of a "perfect elimination ordering" for a bipartite graph between vertices and colors. So distance is crucial.

Can we solve the elimination process with local counts using a queue and DSU? Since each removal affects O(K) neighbors, but K can be large; however total N 250k, O(N log N) possible if we use segment trees to query counts in intervals.

For each position i, we need to know:
- count of remaining positions j in [i-K, i+K] with B_j = B_i.
- count of removed positions j in [i-K, i+K] with A_j = B_i.
If either >=1 (excluding i itself for remaining), i is removable.

When we remove i, we need update these counts for all positions whose window includes i. That's O(K) per removal, too slow if K large. Need a more global approach.

Maybe we can process by values. For each value v, consider positions with B_i=v. A remaining position i with B_i=v is removable via before-witness if there is another remaining position with B=v within K. This is equivalent to the nearest remaining same-B position distance <=K. We can maintain for each value a sorted set of remaining positions with that B. When removing i, remove i from set of B_i. Then for neighboring positions of same value, their nearest distance may increase. We can update only the affected neighbors? Removing i from a sorted set affects the gap between predecessor and successor; only the positions adjacent to i in the sorted order may lose their nearest neighbor. So we can maintain removability via before-witness efficiently with sorted sets.

Similarly, after-witness: when removing i, we add A_i to the set of removed positions with that A value. This can make positions with B = A_i removable if they are within K of i. We need to find all remaining positions with B=A_i within K of i that are not already removable, and add them to queue. This could be many over all removals, but each position can be added once. We can use sorted sets of remaining positions for each B value to query range [i-K, i+K] and mark them. When a position becomes removable, we push it. If it later becomes unremovable due to removal of its before-witness, we need handle. But if we use a queue and process, a position might be pushed but no longer removable when popped. We can recheck.

This suggests a greedy elimination algorithm:
- Maintain for each value v:
   * sorted set Rem[v] of positions i not yet removed with B_i = v.
   * sorted set RemA[v] of positions i removed with A_i = v (after-witnesses).
- For each position i, compute if it is currently removable:
   * before: exists j in Rem[B_i], j != i, |j-i|<=K.
   * after: exists j in RemA[B_i], |j-i|<=K.
- Initialize queue with all removable unmatched positions.
- While queue not empty:
   pop i; if i already removed or not unmatched, skip.
   if i is not currently removable (recheck), skip.
   remove i:
      - remove i from Rem[B_i].
      - add i to RemA[A_i].
      - Mark removed.
      - Now, removing i from Rem[B_i] may affect before-removability of other positions with B=B_i. Specifically, the predecessor and successor of i in Rem[B_i] (if any) may lose their nearest same-B neighbor if i was their only one. We need recheck them and if they are no longer removable, they might still be in queue; we can just leave them (they'll be skipped). Similarly, when a position is removed, it is removed from Rem[v] and from NotQueued[v] if present.
