In the removal process, a position i is removable if B_i has a witness before (remaining B) or after (removed A). Removing i can block positions j that have B_j = B_i and whose only before-witness was i, and no after-witness. Such j must be removed before i. - If j's only witness for B_j is i (remaining B_i = B_j), then j must be removed before i. Edge j -> i (j before i in removal order? Removal order from last to first: removing earlier means later in forward? Let's define removal order r: first removed is last in forward. If j must be removed before i in removal order? - If j has an after-witness i (removed A_i = B_j), then i must be removed before j (since i is after j in forward? In removal order, i removed before j means i is later in forward, so at time j processed, i unprocessed with A_i=B_j. Yes). So edge i -> j in removal order.

Thus we have a dependency graph: For each unmatched position i, to be removable, it needs either a before-witness (remaining B) or after-witness (removed A). If we choose a witness:
- before-witness j (B_j=B_i): j must be removed after i (because j remains to witness i). Edge i -> j? Wait removal order: i removed before j? - after-witness j (A_j=B_i): j removed before i. Edge j -> i.
This is exactly the same as forward but with removal order. We need find an acyclic choice of witnesses.

This is a classic problem of finding a topological ordering with each vertex having at least one incoming/outgoing edge. It can be solved by repeatedly finding a vertex with no outgoing dependencies? Maybe we can model as a SAT problem and use the fact that the graph is an interval graph to solve with 2-SAT? Let's see if the dependency choice can be formulated as 2-SAT. For each position i, we need at least one witness. This is an OR over possible witnesses. Each witness choice imposes an order relation between i and j. Order relations are transitive; a set of order constraints is acyclic iff there is a topological order. This is equivalent to the existence of a total order satisfying a set of Horn? Not 2-SAT.

The condition for an ordering to exist is equivalent to: There is no nonempty set S of mismatched positions such that for every i in S, all witnesses for B_i are in S and are before? This is a Hall-like condition. Let's derive a necessary and sufficient condition using the concept of "closed sets".

In the forward process, suppose we have a set S of positions that are not yet processed at some time. If no position in S can be processed, then for every i in S:
- No processed position j with B_j = B_i within K.
- No other unprocessed position j in S with A_j = B_i within K? Wait unprocessed witness for i can be j with A_j = B_i (j after i), not B_j. In forward, unprocessed positions have A, processed have B. So if S is the set of unprocessed positions, a position i in S can be processed if there exists j in S, j!=i, with A_j = B_i within K (after-witness) OR j processed with B_j = B_i within K (before-witness). If no processed witness and no unprocessed A_j=B_i, stuck. Thus a "closed" set S (unprocessed) is one where for every i in S, B_i does not appear as A_j for any j in S within K, and does not appear as B_j for any processed j within K. Since processed set is complement of S (plus initially matched). This is complex.

But if we consider the last processed position (the one processed at the very end), before it, S={i}. It can be processed iff there is a processed j with B_j=B_i (since no unprocessed A_j). Thus the last position must have B_i appearing among processed positions, i.e., among positions processed earlier. This is a necessary condition. Recursively, if we remove the last position, the condition for the new last is similar. This is the removal process.

This SCC condition seems very promising and is polynomial! Let's formalize.

We construct a directed graph H on positions 1..N. For each position i (mismatched or matched? Matched positions have no need but can be witnesses. We include all positions as vertices). For each ordered pair (i,j) with i != j and |i-j| <= K:
- If A_j = B_i, add edge i -> j. (j can be after i because j initially has B_i)
- If B_j = B_i, add edge j -> i. (j can be before i because after processing j it has B_i)
Actually if both conditions hold for same j, add both edges? If A_j=B_i and B_j=B_i, then j is initially matched with value B_i, so it can be before i; edge j->i suffices. The edge i->j (j after) is also valid but not needed. Adding both might create cycles but SCC condition should still work? If we add both, a matched vertex could create cycles; but matched vertices in SCC make it okay. However, extra edges might merge SCCs and potentially make an impossible SCC have a matched vertex? If a matched vertex has edges both ways, it could merge with mismatched SCC and make it "okay" even if no actual witness? But if there is an edge from matched j to i (B_j=B_i), then j is a valid before-witness, so it should help. If there is edge i->j (A_j=B_i), since j matched A_j=B_j=B_i, j also has B_i initially, so it can be before i; the edge i->j (after) is not needed. Adding it might create a cycle but the matched vertex can break it. It shouldn't cause false positives because if j matched and A_j=B_i, then j is a valid witness before i, so i can be satisfied. Thus adding both is safe. But to keep SCCs meaningful, we can add edge j->i when B_j=B_i (including matched), and edge i->j when A_j=B_i and B_j != B_i? Actually if A_j=B_i and B_j=B_i, j is matched and can be before; edge j->i already added by B_j=B_i. So no need i->j. If A_j=B_i and B_j != B_i, j is mismatched and can be after; add i->j. If B_j=B_i and A_j != B_i, j mismatched and can be before; add j->i. If both A_j=B_i and B_j=B_i, add j->i (before). This avoids unnecessary edges. Let's define:
For each i (mismatched), for each neighbor j:
- If A_j == B_i and B_j != B_i: add edge i -> j.
- If B_j == B_i and A_j != B_i: add edge j -> i.
- If A_j == B_i and B_j == B_i: add edge j -> i (j is matched, before).
But what if i itself is matched? We don't need to process it; it can still have edges as witness for others via the above conditions when considering other i. Now, implement SCC on graph with up to N + total segment nodes. Total segment nodes for each value list: if we build a segment tree recursively for each list, sum of nodes about 2*sum(len) = 2N. Plus N positions = 3N. Edges:
- Segment tree parent->child: about total_nodes - N = N.

- After-witness range edges: for each mismatched position i, add O(log len) edges to segment nodes. Total O(N log N) ~ 4.5M. This is okay but memory for adjacency list of 4.5M directed edges in Python might be high (each edge as int in list ~28 bytes -> 126MB, plus lists overhead). Need optimize.

We can reduce after-witness edges by using a segment tree over positions with a single global tree? Let's think.

Instead of per-value segment trees, we can use a segment tree over positions 1..N where each leaf is a position. To add edge i -> all positions j in [i-K,i+K] with A_j=v and B_j!=v, we need filter by value. That's not a simple range in position. Per-value list segment tree is natural.

Can we avoid building all after-witness edges by using SCC condition only? Maybe we can compute SCCs with a DSU plus Tarjan on a smaller graph where after-witness edges are represented by connecting B-value components to A-value components. Let's explore.

We have before-witness components (bidirectional same-B within K). Let's call them B-components. Each position belongs to a B-component. After-witness edge from position i (in B-component C with value v) to position j (in B-component D) exists if A_j=v and B_j!=v and |i-j|<=K. This is an edge from C to D. For SCC, we can contract B-components first (since they are strongly connected via bidirectional edges). Then we have a directed graph between B-components. Number of B-components <= N. We need add edges from C to D if there exists i in C and j in D with B_i = A_j, B_j != B_i, |i-j|<=K. This is a bipartite reachability between components based on positions. We can compute for each component C and value v whether C has an outgoing edge to any component containing a position with A=v. This is like: for each position j with A_j=v, it can be reached from any position i with B_i=v within K. So for each value v, consider the set of positions with B=v (source positions) and positions with A=v and B!=v (target positions). If a target j is within K of a source i, then the B-component of i has an edge to the B-component of j. This is a range intersection problem. We can add edges between components using a sweep line and segment tree, but number of edges could still be large.

Maybe we can compute SCCs of the component graph using interval edges. For each value v, source positions (B=v) and target positions (A=v, B!=v). For each target j, it creates edges from all source components that have a source position within [j-K, j+K]. This is a range of source positions. We can use a segment tree over source positions for each value to add edges from range to target component. This is similar but now nodes are B-components instead of positions. Still O(N log N).

But we can use arrays (forward star) to save memory. However, coding SCC with 4.5M edges in Python is challenging but possible with iterative Kosaraju? Need two passes over reverse graph; storing reverse edges doubles memory. Tarjan requires adjacency only, no reverse, but recursion depth issue; iterative Tarjan complex.

We can reduce edges by using segment tree but not adding reverse for SCC? Tarjan only needs forward adjacency. Implement iterative Tarjan with adjacency list of lists; 4.5M ints in lists ~ 126MB, plus segment nodes ~ 750k lists overhead huge (list per node). Maybe we can avoid segment tree edges by using a simpler SCC computation with DSU and BFS? Let's see if the SCC condition can be checked without full SCC. The graph has a special structure: before-witness edges are bidirectional within B-components. After-witness edges go from a B-component of value v to B-components of positions with A=v. This is a directed graph on B-components where edges are labeled by values. We can compute SCCs of this component graph. The number of B-components <= N. The number of possible edges between components could be large, but maybe we can compute SCCs using reachability via value sets.

For each value v, let S_v be the set of B-components that contain at least one position with B=v (source components). Let T_v be the set of B-components that contain at least one position with A=v and B!=v (target components). There is an edge from source component C to target component D if there exists i in C with B_i=v and j in D with A_j=v, B_j!=v, |i-j|<=K. This is a geometric edge. To compute SCCs, we need transitive closure of these edges. This is like a bipartite graph between values and components. Maybe we can use BFS with interval sets.

Given the complexity, perhaps the official solution uses a much simpler condition that I'm missing. Let's search memory one final time: ARC124D "Yet Another Sorting" maybe solution: "Create a graph with vertices 1..N. Add edge i->j if |i-j|<=K and A_j = B_i. Then answer is Yes iff every weakly connected component has a cycle?" No.

Wait, the operation "change A_i to A_j" with |i-j|<=K. There is a known result: It is possible iff for every i, A_i = B_i or there exists j with |i-j|<=K and A_j = B_i or B_j = B_i, AND there is no cycle in a graph where edges are i -> j if A_j = B_i and j -> i if B_j = B_i. This is exactly our SCC condition but maybe they check cycles in a graph with edges chosen by nearest occurrences. The SCC condition we derived might be the intended one. Let's see if we can implement it more simply by adding edges to nearest positions only and checking SCC? In unreachable example, nearest after-witness for pos2 was pos3 (edge 2->3), nearest before-witness for pos3 was pos2 (edge 3->2), cycle. If we add only nearest witnesses, SCC still captures cycle. Maybe adding only nearest same-B and nearest A=v is sufficient for SCC condition? Let's test.

For before-witness, we add edges between adjacent same-B positions within K (nearest). For after-witness, for each position i with B_i=v, we need an outgoing edge to some position j with A_j=v within K. If we add edge to the nearest such j (left and right), would that preserve SCCs? Suppose there are multiple after-witnesses, and the nearest one is in the same SCC as i, but a farther one is outside, providing the only external edge. If we only add nearest, we might miss external edge and falsely say No. Example: i has after-witness j1 inside its SCC and j2 outside, both within K. The SCC already has internal cycle; external edge to j2 is needed to satisfy condition. If we omit j2, SCC might have no external -> false No. Could this happen? Let's construct. i in SCC C, j1 in C with A=v, j2 outside with A=v. Since j1 in C, there is a path from i to j1 and back. The after-witness edge i->j1 is internal. The edge i->j2 would be external. If we only add nearest (say j1), C might have no external, but full graph has external via j2. So we need all after-witnesses or at least one external if exists. Adding nearest left and right might still miss j2 if j1 is nearer on both sides? If j2 is outside but within K, and j1 is between i and j2? If j1 is in same SCC, j2 could be farther but still within K. Nearest left/right would be j1, missing j2. So not enough.

Could we add edges to the farthest after-witness as well? To capture external edges, maybe add edges to the leftmost and rightmost in range. If there is any external witness, the leftmost or rightmost might be external? Not necessarily; external could be in middle. But if j1 and j2 both in range, the leftmost/rightmost could be internal, missing middle external. Example positions: i at 0, j1 at -1, j2 at 1, K=2. Both within range, leftmost j1 internal, rightmost j2 external. Adding both leftmost and rightmost captures j2. If external is in middle between two internal? Positions: i=0, internal at -1 and 1, external at 0? cannot same. External at 0? no. If there are internal on both sides farther? leftmost/rightmost would be internal, external in middle? Suppose internal at -2 and 2, external at -1, K=3. Leftmost -2 internal, rightmost 2 internal, external -1 missed. Is that possible with j1,j2 internal in same SCC? Yes. So need all.

Thus segment tree range edges seem necessary for exactness.

But maybe we can avoid Tarjan by using iterative Kosaraju with forward-star arrays and a stack. Let's design.

Graph nodes:
- 0..N-1: positions.
- Segment tree nodes: for each value v, build a segment tree over list_A[v]. We'll assign node IDs.

Building per-value segment trees:
For each value v (1..N), let arr = list of positions where A[pos]=v and B[pos]!=v. We need a segment tree over this list. We can build recursively:
```
def build(l,r):
    id = new_node()
    if r-l==1:
        leaf_id = arr[l]  # use position node directly as leaf? To avoid extra leaf node, we can make segment node point to position node.
    else:
        mid...
        left=build(l,mid); right=build(mid,r)
        add_edge(id, left); add_edge(id, right)
    return id
```
If we use position nodes as leaves, then an edge from i to a segment node covering a range reaches all position leaves. We don't need a separate leaf node. For a segment of length 1, the node ID can be the position ID itself? But then internal nodes also need IDs. We can create internal nodes only; for leaf, return position ID. Then add_edge(parent, child) where child may be position or internal. This saves nodes. Total internal nodes for each list of size m is m-1 (if built as a binary tree) or about 2m. If we build recursively splitting until length1, number of internal nodes = m-1. Sum over lists <= N. Great! Total nodes = N + (sum over values max(0, len(list_A[v])-1)) <= 2N.

Build function for a list:
```
def build_seg(arr, l, r):
    if r-l == 1:
        return arr[l]  # position node
    mid = (l+r)//2
    left = build_seg(arr,l,mid)
    right = build_seg(arr,mid,r)
    node = new_node()
    add_edge(node, left)
    add_edge(node, right)
    return node
```
Then for a query range [ql, qr) in arr, we need add edge from source position i to segment nodes covering the range. Standard:
```
def add_range(src, node, l, r, ql, qr):
    if ql <= l and r <= qr:
        add_edge(src, node)
        return
    mid...
    if ql < mid: add_range(src, left, l, mid, ql, qr)
    if mid < qr: add_range(src, right, mid, r, ql, qr)
```
But if node is a position leaf (r-l==1), and range covers it, add_edge(src, position). This works.

However, if we build a segment tree for each value list, the structure depends on list. We need store root and node ranges to query. We can build recursively and during build store for each internal node its l,r and children. Since total nodes O(N), we can store arrays
</think>
